Yeah, um… I guess what brought me in is I don’t really feel like myself lately. The things I usually just do—like going on photography walks, helping mentor the kids, even keeping my calendar clean and organized—those used to kind of keep me grounded.

Now they just feel… flat. Heavy. Like I look at the camera bag and think about going out, and then I don’t. Same with the mentoring stuff, and I used to be the person people came to for organizing things—funny, because that’s basically my job too—but lately I just stop before I start. I’m not initiating things the way I normally would, and that’s what made me think maybe I should come in.

as vivid and clear as normal perception.\n  manifestation: Sometimes, usually when I'm alone at my desk or driving back from an appointment, I hear my name or a short comment like 'fix the schedule' as clearly as if someone is beside me, but when I look there’s no one there.\n- key: S033\n  name: Elevated_Irritable_Mood\n  description: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.\n  manifestation: I have stretches where I feel unusually upbeat and sure everything is clicking, then a tiny change like someone moving a meeting can make me sharp with people in a way that surprises me.\n- key: S034\n  name: Increased_Activity\n  description: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.\n  manifestation: During those stretches I start rebuilding work templates, making care plans, emailing relatives, and reorganizing files all at once, like sitting still would mean I’m dropping the ball.\n- key: S035\n  name: Inflated_Self_Esteem\n  description: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).\n  manifestation: I’ll feel like I’m the only one who can straighten out the doctors, the family, and the office, and I start believing my system is better than anyone else’s judgment.\n- key: S036\n  name: Decreased_Sleep\n  description: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).\n  manifestation: There have been nights when I sleep two or three hours, wake up before dawn, and feel ready to answer emails or update appointment lists instead of feeling tired.\n- key: S037\n  name: Pressured_Speech\n  description: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.\n  manifestation: My wife and coworkers tell me I talk so fast during those periods that I jump from medical forms to work deadlines to mentoring ideas before they can get a word in.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: psychotic_symptoms and manic_episode occur concurrently\n  manifestation: During my high-energy stretches, the certainty that messages or calendar problems meant something special would get stronger, and I would sometimes hear those short comments while I was running on very little sleep.\n- key: additional_requirement_2\n  name: Additional Requirement 2\n  description: manic_episode is present for the majority of the total duration of the active and residual portions of the illness\n  manifestation: For most of this whole period, I’ve been cycling back into those driven, overconfident stretches where I sleep less and take on too much, even if some weeks are quieter.\n- key: additional_requirement_3\n  name: Additional Requirement 3\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur for 2 or more weeks in the absence of a major mood episode\n  manifestation: There have been calm weeks when I wasn’t revved up or sleeping less, but for more than a couple of weeks I still heard my name or felt convinced ordinary work comments were coded tests.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: My work has stayed intact overall, but I’ve missed calendar details and had to double-check tasks that used to be automatic. I’ve also pulled back from photography outings and mentoring because I don’t feel as present or reliable.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 years and 3 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about a year and three months, starting after my family member’s health and behavior changed and I took on more coordination.\n- key: psychotic_symptoms_duration\n  name: Psychotic Symptoms Duration\n  description: 10 months\n  manifestation: The voices and the fixed worries about signs in calendars or meetings have been happening for roughly ten months.\n- key: manic_episode_duration\n  name: Manic Episode Duration\n  description: 11 months\n  manifestation: The elevated, driven stretches with less sleep and too much confidence have been recurring for about eleven months.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 46 / executive_secretary_or_executive_administrative_assistant / some_college\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S023\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it excessive. I mean, I worry about normal stuff—work things, whether I forgot to respond to someone, family things—but I’m not sure it’s “a lot of different things” in that way.\n\nSometimes I get stuck thinking about whether I handled something right, like if an email sounded too short. But then again, in my job, details kind of matter, so it’s hard to tell what’s worry and what’s just… being careful. What do you mean by excessive?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control the worrying once it starts?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, I guess? It depends what it’s about. Like if it’s an email or a scheduling thing, I’ll replay it in my head, and then I’ll remember some completely separate thing, like whether I put the parking receipt in the right folder, which sounds ridiculous out loud.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d say I can’t control it. Usually I can get pulled into something else if I have to. It’s more like my mind keeps circling back unless something interrupts me. Does that count as hard to control?\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt sad or depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “sad” most of the day. Depressed is such a heavy word, you know? I have days where I feel kind of low or like I’m moving through wet cement, but then I’ll get distracted by something—like reorganizing a meeting agenda or noticing the office printer is doing that thing where it leaves a line down the page—and then I’m not really thinking about my mood.\n\nSo… nearly every day, most of the day? as clearly as if someone is beside me, but when I look there’s no one there.\n- key: S033\n  name: Elevated_Irritable_Mood\n  description: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.\n  manifestation: I have stretches where I feel unusually upbeat and sure everything is clicking, then a tiny change like someone moving a meeting can make me sharp with people in a way that surprises me.\n- key: S034\n  name: Increased_Activity\n  description: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.\n  manifestation: During those stretches I start rebuilding work templates, making care plans, emailing relatives, and reorganizing files all at once, like sitting still would mean I’m dropping the ball.\n- key: S035\n  name: Inflated_Self_Esteem\n  description: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).\n  manifestation: I’ll feel like I’m the only one who can straighten out the doctors, the family, and the office, and I start believing my system is better than anyone else’s judgment.\n- key: S036\n  name: Decreased_Sleep\n  description: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).\n  manifestation: There have been nights when I sleep two or three hours, wake up before dawn, and feel ready to answer emails or update appointment lists instead of feeling tired.\n- key: S037\n  name: Pressured_Speech\n  description: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.\n  manifestation: My wife and coworkers tell me I talk so fast during those periods that I jump from medical forms to work deadlines to mentoring ideas before they can get a word in.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: psychotic_symptoms and manic_episode occur concurrently\n  manifestation: During my high-energy stretches, the certainty that messages or calendar problems meant something special would get stronger, and I would sometimes hear those short comments while I was running on very little sleep.\n- key: additional_requirement_2\n  name: Additional Requirement 2\n  description: manic_episode is present for the majority of the total duration of the active and residual portions of the illness\n  manifestation: For most of this whole period, I’ve been cycling back into those driven, overconfident stretches where I sleep less and take on too much, even if some weeks are quieter.\n- key: additional_requirement_3\n  name: Additional Requirement 3\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur for 2 or more weeks in the absence of a major mood episode\n  manifestation: There have been calm weeks when I wasn’t revved up or sleeping less, but for more than a couple of weeks I still heard my name or felt convinced ordinary work comments were coded tests.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: My work has stayed intact overall, but I’ve missed calendar details and had to double-check tasks that used to be automatic. I’ve also pulled back from photography outings and mentoring because I don’t feel as present or reliable.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 years and 3 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about a year and three months, starting after my family member’s health and behavior changed and I took on more coordination.\n- key: psychotic_symptoms_duration\n  name: Psychotic Symptoms Duration\n  description: 10 months\n  manifestation: The voices and the fixed worries about signs in calendars or meetings have been happening for roughly ten months.\n- key: manic_episode_duration\n  name: Manic Episode Duration\n  description: 11 months\n  manifestation: The elevated, driven stretches with less sleep and too much confidence have been recurring for about eleven months.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 46 / executive_secretary_or_executive_administrative_assistant / some_college\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S023\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you felt down, depressed, or hopeless?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. “Hopeless” feels like a really specific word, and I don’t know that I’d use that. Down, maybe here and there, but then I get pulled into whatever’s in front of me—like a calendar issue or somebody needing a document reformatted—and then I’m just dealing with that.\n\nSo… in the past two weeks, I guess I’ve had moments of feeling down, but I don’t know if it’s enough to say yes the way you mean it.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you had much less interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s actually part of why I came in. The things that usually make me feel like myself have just felt kind of flat lately.\n\nLike, I usually like going out for photography walks—there’s this one street where the light hits the buildings in a really specific way around late afternoon, and I’ll take way too many pictures of the same fire escape, which sounds boring, but it’s calming for me. And mentoring the kids too, that usually matters to me. Even keeping my calendar clean, which I know is a weird thing to say I “enjoy,” but I do like when everything is organized.\n\nLately I just… don’t start those things. They feel heavy, like there’s no spark in them. I don’t know if it’s “much less pleasure” exactly, but yes, less interest, less pull toward doing them.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically tense or unable to relax?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “often.” I mean, I get tense sometimes, sure, but then I’m also sitting at a desk a lot, and those office chairs—there’s one in the conference room that sinks down slowly while you’re sitting in it, which drives me crazy because then you’re suddenly lower than everyone else at the table.\n\nBut physically tense… I haven’t really been tracking it. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.9, F43.23, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess what brought me in is I don’t really feel like myself lately. The things I usually just do—like going on photography walks, helping mentor the kids, even keeping my calendar clean and organized—those used to kind of keep me grounded.\n\nNow they just feel… flat. Heavy. Like I look at the camera bag and think about going out, and then I don’t. Same with the mentoring stuff, and I used to be the person people came to for organizing things—funny, because that’s basically my job too—but lately I just stop before I start. I’m not initiating things the way I normally would, and that’s what made me think maybe I should come in.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Um… I’d say about a year and three months, give or take. It sort of started after a family member’s health and behavior changed, and I ended up taking on more of the coordinating—appointments, calls, keeping track of things. Which, I mean, I’m usually good at that kind of thing, that’s my whole work life too, but somewhere in there it started feeling different. So… yeah, roughly fifteen months.\n\nDoctor: Do these changes feel mainly driven by the situation with your family member?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, it lines up with that, timing-wise, so part of me says yes, probably. But then I think… lots of people deal with family stuff and still manage to keep their routines, you know?\n\nAnd it’s weird because organizing things is usually where I’m solid. Like at work, if someone needs a meeting moved or ten people coordinated, I can usually make that happen. I used to even color-code things—probably too much, honestly. My sister used to joke about it. So when it started feeling hard, I kind of blamed the family situation, but I don’t know if that explains all of it. It feels connected, but I can’t say it’s only that.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this down or flat most days for the past two years?\n\nPatient: Most days… yeah, I think flat is the better word than down. Like, not crying all the time or anything, but the stuff that normally makes me feel like me—going out with my camera, mentoring the kids, even just keeping my calendar neat—started feeling heavy.\n\nI just stopped starting things, if that makes sense. I’d look at the camera bag and then somehow end up sorting old emails instead, which is not even useful, just… I don’t know. But two years? I’m not sure it’s been that long. It feels more like it’s been going on a long while, but I don’t think a full two years.\n\nDoctor: Before this past 15 months, have you ever had another period lasting at least two weeks when you felt similarly flat or lost interest in the things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Before this past year or so? I don’t think I had it like this, not for two weeks straight where it really stood out. I mean, I’ve had times where I got bored with things—like I’d buy a camera lens and then not use it for a while, which my friend used to tease me about because I’d research it for weeks and then it would just sit there—but that wasn’t the same.\n\nThis time it’s more like the things that usually make me feel like myself, like going on photography walks, mentoring the kids, even just keeping my calendar clean… they started feeling flat and heavy. And I just stopped initiating them. That’s really what made me come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it excessive. I mean, I worry about normal stuff—work things, whether I forgot to respond to someone, family things—but I’m not sure it’s “a lot of different things” in that way.\n\nSometimes I get stuck thinking about whether I handled something right, like if an email sounded too short. But then again, in my job, details kind of matter, so it’s hard to tell what’s worry and what’s just… being careful. What do you mean by excessive?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control the worrying once it starts?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, I guess? It depends what it’s about. Like if it’s an email or a scheduling thing, I’ll replay it in my head, and then I’ll remember some completely separate thing, like whether I put the parking receipt in the right folder, which sounds ridiculous out loud.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d say I can’t control it. Usually I can get pulled into something else if I have to. It’s more like my mind keeps circling back unless something interrupts me. Does that count as hard to control?\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt sad or depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “sad” most of the day. Depressed is such a heavy word, you know? I have days where I feel kind of low or like I’m moving through wet cement, but then I’ll get distracted by something—like reorganizing a meeting agenda or noticing the office printer is doing that thing where it leaves a line down the page—and then I’m not really thinking about my mood.\n\nSo… nearly every day, most of the day? I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to say yes if it’s not exactly that.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you felt down, depressed, or hopeless?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. “Hopeless” feels like a really specific word, and I don’t know that I’d use that. Down, maybe here and there, but then I get pulled into whatever’s in front of me—like a calendar issue or somebody needing a document reformatted—and then I’m just dealing with that.\n\nSo… in the past two weeks, I guess I’ve had moments of feeling down, but I don’t know if it’s enough to say yes the way you mean it.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you had much less interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s actually part of why I came in. They feel heavy, like there’s no spark in them. I don’t know if it’s “much less pleasure” exactly, but yes, less interest, less pull toward doing them.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically tense or unable to relax?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “often.” I mean, I get tense sometimes, sure, but then I’m also sitting at a desk a lot, and those office chairs—there’s one in the conference room that sinks down slowly while you’re sitting in it, which drives me crazy because then you’re suddenly lower than everyone else at the table.\n\nBut physically tense… I haven’t really been tracking it. I think… probably not the same way, no. It’s hard to separate it out because it’s been tangled up with that for a while now.

It started after my family member’s health and behavior changed, and then I kind of became the person coordinating things—calls, appointments, checking in, keeping track of who said what. It’s funny, at work I’m good at coordinating calendars and details, so everyone assumes that means I can just do it endlessly in my personal life too, like I’m a walking spreadsheet.

But yeah… this has been going on about a year and three months, I’d say. Since that shift happened. So if none of that had happened, I don’t know that I’d be feeling like this, at least not in this same way.

I mean, I worry about normal stuff—work things, whether I forgot to respond to someone, family things—but I’m not sure it’s “a lot of different things” in that way.\n\nSometimes I get stuck thinking about whether I handled something right, like if an email sounded too short. But then again, in my job, details kind of matter, so it’s hard to tell what’s worry and what’s just… being careful. What do you mean by excessive?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control the worrying once it starts?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, I guess? It depends what it’s about. Like if it’s an email or a scheduling thing, I’ll replay it in my head, and then I’ll remember some completely separate thing, like whether I put the parking receipt in the right folder, which sounds ridiculous out loud.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d say I can’t control it. Usually I can get pulled into something else if I have to. It’s more like my mind keeps circling back unless something interrupts me. Does that count as hard to control?\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt sad or depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “sad” most of the day. Depressed is such a heavy word, you know? I have days where I feel kind of low or like I’m moving through wet cement, but then I’ll get distracted by something—like reorganizing a meeting agenda or noticing the office printer is doing that thing where it leaves a line down the page—and then I’m not really thinking about my mood.\n\nSo… nearly every day, most of the day? I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to say yes if it’s not exactly that.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you felt down, depressed, or hopeless?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. “Hopeless” feels like a really specific word, and I don’t know that I’d use that. Down, maybe here and there, but then I get pulled into whatever’s in front of me—like a calendar issue or somebody needing a document reformatted—and then I’m just dealing with that.\n\nSo… in the past two weeks, I guess I’ve had moments of feeling down, but I don’t know if it’s enough to say yes the way you mean it.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you had much less interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s actually part of why I came in. The things that usually make me feel like myself have just felt kind of flat lately.\n\nLike, I usually like going out for photography walks—there’s this one street where the light hits the buildings in a really specific way around late afternoon, and I’ll take way too many pictures of the same fire escape, which sounds boring, but it’s calming for me. And mentoring the kids too, that usually matters to me. Even keeping my calendar clean, which I know is a weird thing to say I “enjoy,” but I do like when everything is organized.\n\nLately I just… don’t start those things. They feel heavy, like there’s no spark in them. I don’t know if it’s “much less pleasure” exactly, but yes, less interest, less pull toward doing them.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically tense or unable to relax?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “often.” I mean, I get tense sometimes, sure, but then I’m also sitting at a desk a lot, and those office chairs—there’s one in the conference room that sinks down slowly while you’re sitting in it, which drives me crazy because then you’re suddenly lower than everyone else at the table.\n\nBut physically tense… I haven’t really been tracking it. Unable to relax, maybe sometimes, but I’m not sure if that’s what you mean. I guess I’d have to think about it more.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you been feeling unusually tired or low on energy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. “Unusually tired” is hard to judge because I feel like everyone at work is tired all the time, you know? The office has those fluorescent lights that make the afternoon feel longer than it is, and by the time I’m sorting emails or fixing someone’s travel itinerary for the third time, it’s like… of course I’m tired.\n\nBut over the past two weeks specifically? I haven’t really been measuring my energy. Some days I feel kind of drained, but I don’t know if it’s more than usual or just normal life catching up.\n\nDoctor: If the family situation had never happened, do you think you would still be feeling this same way?\n\nPatient: I think… probably not the same way, no. It’s hard to separate it out because it’s been tangled up with that for a while now.\n\nIt started after my family member’s health and behavior changed, and then I kind of became the person coordinating things—calls, appointments, checking in, keeping track of who said what. It’s funny, at work I’m good at coordinating calendars and details, so everyone assumes that means I can just do it endlessly in my personal life too, like I’m a walking spreadsheet.\n\nBut yeah… this has been going on about a year and three months, I’d say. Since that shift happened. So if none of that had happened, I don’t know that I’d be feeling like this, at least not in this same way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Mostly, yeah, I think it’s tied to that ongoing family situation. But what made me actually come in wasn’t just “I’m stressed,” because everybody says that and then you feel silly bringing it up.

Like, not crying all the time or anything, but the stuff that normally makes me feel like me—going out with my camera, mentoring the kids, even just keeping my calendar neat—started feeling heavy.\n\nI just stopped starting things, if that makes sense. I’d look at the camera bag and then somehow end up sorting old emails instead, which is not even useful, just… I don’t know. But two years? I’m not sure it’s been that long. It feels more like it’s been going on a long while, but I don’t think a full two years.\n\nDoctor: Before this past 15 months, have you ever had another period lasting at least two weeks when you felt similarly flat or lost interest in the things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Before this past year or so? I don’t think I had it like this, not for two weeks straight where it really stood out. I mean, I’ve had times where I got bored with things—like I’d buy a camera lens and then not use it for a while, which my friend used to tease me about because I’d research it for weeks and then it would just sit there—but that wasn’t the same.\n\nThis time it’s more like the things that usually make me feel like myself, like going on photography walks, mentoring the kids, even just keeping my calendar clean… they started feeling flat and heavy. And I just stopped initiating them. That’s really what made me come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it excessive. I mean, I worry about normal stuff—work things, whether I forgot to respond to someone, family things—but I’m not sure it’s “a lot of different things” in that way.\n\nSometimes I get stuck thinking about whether I handled something right, like if an email sounded too short. But then again, in my job, details kind of matter, so it’s hard to tell what’s worry and what’s just… being careful. What do you mean by excessive?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control the worrying once it starts?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, I guess? It depends what it’s about. Like if it’s an email or a scheduling thing, I’ll replay it in my head, and then I’ll remember some completely separate thing, like whether I put the parking receipt in the right folder, which sounds ridiculous out loud.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d say I can’t control it. Usually I can get pulled into something else if I have to. It’s more like my mind keeps circling back unless something interrupts me. Does that count as hard to control?\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt sad or depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “sad” most of the day. Depressed is such a heavy word, you know? I have days where I feel kind of low or like I’m moving through wet cement, but then I’ll get distracted by something—like reorganizing a meeting agenda or noticing the office printer is doing that thing where it leaves a line down the page—and then I’m not really thinking about my mood.\n\nSo… nearly every day, most of the day? I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to say yes if it’s not exactly that.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you felt down, depressed, or hopeless?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. “Hopeless” feels like a really specific word, and I don’t know that I’d use that. Down, maybe here and there, but then I get pulled into whatever’s in front of me—like a calendar issue or somebody needing a document reformatted—and then I’m just dealing with that.\n\nSo… in the past two weeks, I guess I’ve had moments of feeling down, but I don’t know if it’s enough to say yes the way you mean it.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you had much less interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s actually part of why I came in. The things that usually make me feel like myself have just felt kind of flat lately.\n\nLike, I usually like going out for photography walks—there’s this one street where the light hits the buildings in a really specific way around late afternoon, and I’ll take way too many pictures of the same fire escape, which sounds boring, but it’s calming for me. And mentoring the kids too, that usually matters to me. Even keeping my calendar clean, which I know is a weird thing to say I “enjoy,” but I do like when everything is organized.\n\nLately I just… don’t start those things. They feel heavy, like there’s no spark in them. I don’t know if it’s “much less pleasure” exactly, but yes, less interest, less pull toward doing them.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically tense or unable to relax?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “often.” I mean, I get tense sometimes, sure, but then I’m also sitting at a desk a lot, and those office chairs—there’s one in the conference room that sinks down slowly while you’re sitting in it, which drives me crazy because then you’re suddenly lower than everyone else at the table.\n\nBut physically tense… I haven’t really been tracking it. Unable to relax, maybe sometimes, but I’m not sure if that’s what you mean. I guess I’d have to think about it more.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you been feeling unusually tired or low on energy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. “Unusually tired” is hard to judge because I feel like everyone at work is tired all the time, you know? The office has those fluorescent lights that make the afternoon feel longer than it is, and by the time I’m sorting emails or fixing someone’s travel itinerary for the third time, it’s like… of course I’m tired.\n\nBut over the past two weeks specifically? I haven’t really been measuring my energy. Some days I feel kind of drained, but I don’t know if it’s more than usual or just normal life catching up.\n\nDoctor: If the family situation had never happened, do you think you would still be feeling this same way?\n\nPatient: I think… probably not the same way, no. It’s hard to separate it out because it’s been tangled up with that for a while now.\n\nIt started after my family member’s health and behavior changed, and then I kind of became the person coordinating things—calls, appointments, checking in, keeping track of who said what. It’s funny, at work I’m good at coordinating calendars and details, so everyone assumes that means I can just do it endlessly in my personal life too, like I’m a walking spreadsheet.\n\nBut yeah… this has been going on about a year and three months, I’d say. Since that shift happened. So if none of that had happened, I don’t know that I’d be feeling like this, at least not in this same way.\n\nDoctor: Has this been making it hard for you to function at work or take care of things you need to do day to day?\n\nPatient: Some, yeah. Not like everything is falling apart. Work is mostly intact—I’m still getting the big things done, meetings still happen, people still get what they need. But I’ve noticed I miss little calendar details sometimes, or I’ll double-check something that used to be automatic, like whether I actually sent the attachment or put the right room number in.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve kind of pulled back. I used to do more photography outings, and I was mentoring a bit, but lately I don’t feel as present or reliable, so I’ve been saying no more than I used to. It’s not dramatic, but it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your loss of interest and heaviness are mainly a reaction to the ongoing family situation?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yeah, I think it’s tied to that ongoing family situation. But what made me actually come in wasn’t just “I’m stressed,” because everybody says that and then you feel silly bringing it up.\n\nIt was more like… the things that usually make me feel like myself started feeling flat and heavy. Like photography walks—I’d usually notice the light or a weird reflection in a window, and lately I just don’t even start. And mentoring the kids, too, I’ve pulled back from initiating that. Even keeping a clean calendar, which sounds boring, but that’s kind of my thing. When that started feeling like dragging furniture across carpet, I thought, okay, maybe this is more than just a bad week.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: If the family situation had never happened, do you think you would still be feeling this same way?\n\nPatient: I think… probably not the same way, no. It’s hard to separate it out because it’s been tangled up with that for a while now.\n\nIt started after my family member’s health and behavior changed, and then I kind of became the person coordinating things—calls, appointments, checking in, keeping track of who said what. It’s funny, at work I’m good at coordinating calendars and details, so everyone assumes that means I can just do it endlessly in my personal life too, like I’m a walking spreadsheet.\n\nBut yeah… this has been going on about a year and three months, I’d say. Since that shift happened. So if none of that had happened, I don’t know that I’d be feeling like this, at least not in this same way.\n\nDoctor: Has this been making it hard for you to function at work or take care of things you need to do day to day?\n\nPatient: Some, yeah. Not like everything is falling apart. Work is mostly intact—I’m still getting the big things done, meetings still happen, people still get what they need. But I’ve noticed I miss little calendar details sometimes, or I’ll double-check something that used to be automatic, like whether I actually sent the attachment or put the right room number in.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve kind of pulled back. I used to do more photography outings, and I was mentoring a bit, but lately I don’t feel as present or reliable, so I’ve been saying no more than I used to. It’s not dramatic, but it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your loss of interest and heaviness are mainly a reaction to the ongoing family situation?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yeah, I think it’s tied to that ongoing family situation. But what made me actually come in wasn’t just “I’m stressed,” because everybody says that and then you feel silly bringing it up.\n\nIt was more like… the things that usually make me feel like myself started feeling flat and heavy. Like photography walks—I’d usually notice the light or a weird reflection in a window, and lately I just don’t even start. And mentoring the kids, too, I’ve pulled back from initiating that. Even keeping a clean calendar, which sounds boring, but that’s kind of my thing. When that started feeling like dragging furniture across carpet, I thought, okay, maybe this is more than just a bad week.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring this past year, have you often felt sad?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 46 / executive_secretary_or_executive_administrative_assistant / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I’d say “sad” exactly, or at least not in a clean, obvious way. It’s more like… some days there’s this heaviness, and then other days I’m just going through the motions.

Sad is tricky, because when people say sad I picture crying at a movie or something clear-cut, and that’s not really how I’d describe it. I mean, I’ll be sitting at my desk looking at the calendar color-coding, and suddenly I’m thinking about how I used to care about making things neat, and then I end up reorganizing paper clips instead of answering the question in my own head. So… often? Maybe. I’m not totally sure.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.23, F32.9, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess what brought me in is I don’t really feel like myself lately. The things I usually just do—like going on photography walks, helping mentor the kids, even keeping my calendar clean and organized—those used to kind of keep me grounded.\n\nNow they just feel… flat. Heavy. Like I look at the camera bag and think about going out, and then I don’t. Same with the mentoring stuff, and I used to be the person people came to for organizing things—funny, because that’s basically my job too—but lately I just stop before I start. I’m not initiating things the way I normally would, and that’s what made me think maybe I should come in.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Um… I’d say about a year and three months, give or take. It sort of started after a family member’s health and behavior changed, and I ended up taking on more of the coordinating—appointments, calls, keeping track of things. Which, I mean, I’m usually good at that kind of thing, that’s my whole work life too, but somewhere in there it started feeling different. So… yeah, roughly fifteen months.\n\nDoctor: Do these changes feel mainly driven by the situation with your family member?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, it lines up with that, timing-wise, so part of me says yes, probably. But then I think… lots of people deal with family stuff and still manage to keep their routines, you know?\n\nAnd it’s weird because organizing things is usually where I’m solid. Like at work, if someone needs a meeting moved or ten people coordinated, I can usually make that happen. I used to even color-code things—probably too much, honestly. My sister used to joke about it. So when it started feeling hard, I kind of blamed the family situation, but I don’t know if that explains all of it. It feels connected, but I can’t say it’s only that.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this down or flat most days for the past two years?\n\nPatient: Most days… yeah, I think flat is the better word than down. Like, not crying all the time or anything, but the stuff that normally makes me feel like me—going out with my camera, mentoring the kids, even just keeping my calendar neat—started feeling heavy.\n\nI just stopped starting things, if that makes sense. I’d look at the camera bag and then somehow end up sorting old emails instead, which is not even useful, just… I don’t know. But two years? I’m not sure it’s been that long. It feels more like it’s been going on a long while, but I don’t think a full two years.\n\nDoctor: Before this past 15 months, have you ever had another period lasting at least two weeks when you felt similarly flat or lost interest in the things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Before this past year or so? I don’t think I had it like this, not for two weeks straight where it really stood out. I mean, I’ve had times where I got bored with things—like I’d buy a camera lens and then not use it for a while, which my friend used to tease me about because I’d research it for weeks and then it would just sit there—but that wasn’t the same.\n\nThis time it’s more like the things that usually make me feel like myself, like going on photography walks, mentoring the kids, even just keeping my calendar clean… they started feeling flat and heavy. And I just stopped initiating them. That’s really what made me come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it excessive. I mean, I worry about normal stuff—work things, whether I forgot to respond to someone, family things—but I’m not sure it’s “a lot of different things” in that way.\n\nSometimes I get stuck thinking about whether I handled something right, like if an email sounded too short. But then again, in my job, details kind of matter, so it’s hard to tell what’s worry and what’s just… being careful. What do you mean by excessive?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control the worrying once it starts?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, I guess? It depends what it’s about. Like if it’s an email or a scheduling thing, I’ll replay it in my head, and then I’ll remember some completely separate thing, like whether I put the parking receipt in the right folder, which sounds ridiculous out loud.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d say I can’t control it. Usually I can get pulled into something else if I have to. It’s more like my mind keeps circling back unless something interrupts me. Does that count as hard to control?\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt sad or depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “sad” most of the day. Depressed is such a heavy word, you know? I have days where I feel kind of low or like I’m moving through wet cement, but then I’ll get distracted by something—like reorganizing a meeting agenda or noticing the office printer is doing that thing where it leaves a line down the page—and then I’m not really thinking about my mood.\n\nSo… nearly every day, most of the day? I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to say yes if it’s not exactly that.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you felt down, depressed, or hopeless?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. “Hopeless” feels like a really specific word, and I don’t know that I’d use that. Down, maybe here and there, but then I get pulled into whatever’s in front of me—like a calendar issue or somebody needing a document reformatted—and then I’m just dealing with that.\n\nSo… in the past two weeks, I guess I’ve had moments of feeling down, but I don’t know if it’s enough to say yes the way you mean it.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you had much less interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s actually part of why I came in. The things that usually make me feel like myself have just felt kind of flat lately.\n\nLike, I usually like going out for photography walks—there’s this one street where the light hits the buildings in a really specific way around late afternoon, and I’ll take way too many pictures of the same fire escape, which sounds boring, but it’s calming for me. Even keeping my calendar clean, which I know is a weird thing to say I “enjoy,” but I do like when everything is organized.\n\nLately I just… don’t start those things. They feel heavy, like there’s no spark in them. I don’t know if it’s “much less pleasure” exactly, but yes, less interest, less pull toward doing them.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically tense or unable to relax?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “often.” I mean, I get tense sometimes, sure, but then I’m also sitting at a desk a lot, and those office chairs—there’s one in the conference room that sinks down slowly while you’re sitting in it, which drives me crazy because then you’re suddenly lower than everyone else at the table.\n\nBut physically tense… I haven’t really been tracking it. Unable to relax, maybe sometimes, but I’m not sure if that’s what you mean. I guess I’d have to think about it more.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you been feeling unusually tired or low on energy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. “Unusually tired” is hard to judge because I feel like everyone at work is tired all the time, you know? The office has those fluorescent lights that make the afternoon feel longer than it is, and by the time I’m sorting emails or fixing someone’s travel itinerary for the third time, it’s like… of course I’m tired.\n\nBut over the past two weeks specifically? I haven’t really been measuring my energy. Some days I feel kind of drained, but I don’t know if it’s more than usual or just normal life catching up.\n\nDoctor: If the family situation had never happened, do you think you would still be feeling this same way?\n\nPatient: I think… probably not the same way, no. Yeah… I think so. I mean, when things calm down a little with family, I’ll expect myself to sort of snap back, like, okay, now go take the camera out or text the mentoring group or clean up the calendar tabs. But it still feels weirdly flat.

That’s actually why I came in. The stuff that normally feels like “me”—photography walks, helping with the kids, even keeping my calendar neat—started feeling heavy, like something I had to push myself toward, and then I just stopped starting those things. I can get distracted by all the family noise, sure, but even when it’s quieter, I’m not really reaching for those things the way I used to.

Like if it’s an email or a scheduling thing, I’ll replay it in my head, and then I’ll remember some completely separate thing, like whether I put the parking receipt in the right folder, which sounds ridiculous out loud.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d say I can’t control it. Usually I can get pulled into something else if I have to. It’s more like my mind keeps circling back unless something interrupts me. I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to say yes if it’s not exactly that.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you felt down, depressed, or hopeless?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. “Hopeless” feels like a really specific word, and I don’t know that I’d use that. Down, maybe here and there, but then I get pulled into whatever’s in front of me—like a calendar issue or somebody needing a document reformatted—and then I’m just dealing with that.\n\nSo… in the past two weeks, I guess I’ve had moments of feeling down, but I don’t know if it’s enough to say yes the way you mean it.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you had much less interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s actually part of why I came in. The things that usually make me feel like myself have just felt kind of flat lately.\n\nLike, I usually like going out for photography walks—there’s this one street where the light hits the buildings in a really specific way around late afternoon, and I’ll take way too many pictures of the same fire escape, which sounds boring, but it’s calming for me. Even keeping my calendar clean, which I know is a weird thing to say I “enjoy,” but I do like when everything is organized.\n\nLately I just… don’t start those things. They feel heavy, like there’s no spark in them. Like, not crying all the time or anything, but the stuff that normally makes me feel like me—going out with my camera, mentoring the kids, even just keeping my calendar neat—started feeling heavy.\n\nI just stopped starting things, if that makes sense. I’d look at the camera bag and then somehow end up sorting old emails instead, which is not even useful, just… I don’t know. But two years? I’m not sure it’s been that long. It feels more like it’s been going on a long while, but I don’t think a full two years.\n\nDoctor: Before this past 15 months, have you ever had another period lasting at least two weeks when you felt similarly flat or lost interest in the things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Before this past year or so? I don’t think I had it like this, not for two weeks straight where it really stood out. I mean, I’ve had times where I got bored with things—like I’d buy a camera lens and then not use it for a while, which my friend used to tease me about because I’d research it for weeks and then it would just sit there—but that wasn’t the same.\n\nThis time it’s more like the things that usually make me feel like myself, like going on photography walks, mentoring the kids, even just keeping my calendar clean… they started feeling flat and heavy. And I just stopped initiating them. That’s really what made me come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it excessive. I mean, I worry about normal stuff—work things, whether I forgot to respond to someone, family things—but I’m not sure it’s “a lot of different things” in that way.\n\nSometimes I get stuck thinking about whether I handled something right, like if an email sounded too short. But then again, in my job, details kind of matter, so it’s hard to tell what’s worry and what’s just… being careful. What do you mean by excessive?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control the worrying once it starts?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, I guess? It depends what it’s about. Like if it’s an email or a scheduling thing, I’ll replay it in my head, and then I’ll remember some completely separate thing, like whether I put the parking receipt in the right folder, which sounds ridiculous out loud.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d say I can’t control it. Usually I can get pulled into something else if I have to. It’s more like my mind keeps circling back unless something interrupts me. Does that count as hard to control?\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt sad or depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “sad” most of the day. Depressed is such a heavy word, you know? I have days where I feel kind of low or like I’m moving through wet cement, but then I’ll get distracted by something—like reorganizing a meeting agenda or noticing the office printer is doing that thing where it leaves a line down the page—and then I’m not really thinking about my mood.\n\nSo… nearly every day, most of the day? I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to say yes if it’s not exactly that.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you felt down, depressed, or hopeless?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. “Hopeless” feels like a really specific word, and I don’t know that I’d use that. Down, maybe here and there, but then I get pulled into whatever’s in front of me—like a calendar issue or somebody needing a document reformatted—and then I’m just dealing with that.\n\nSo… in the past two weeks, I guess I’ve had moments of feeling down, but I don’t know if it’s enough to say yes the way you mean it.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you had much less interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s actually part of why I came in. The things that usually make me feel like myself have just felt kind of flat lately.\n\nLike, I usually like going out for photography walks—there’s this one street where the light hits the buildings in a really specific way around late afternoon, and I’ll take way too many pictures of the same fire escape, which sounds boring, but it’s calming for me. And mentoring the kids too, that usually matters to me. Even keeping my calendar clean, which I know is a weird thing to say I “enjoy,” but I do like when everything is organized.\n\nLately I just… don’t start those things. They feel heavy, like there’s no spark in them. I don’t know if it’s “much less pleasure” exactly, but yes, less interest, less pull toward doing them.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically tense or unable to relax?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “often.” I mean, I get tense sometimes, sure, but then I’m also sitting at a desk a lot, and those office chairs—there’s one in the conference room that sinks down slowly while you’re sitting in it, which drives me crazy because then you’re suddenly lower than everyone else at the table.\n\nBut physically tense… I haven’t really been tracking it. Unable to relax, maybe sometimes, but I’m not sure if that’s what you mean. I guess I’d have to think about it more.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you been feeling unusually tired or low on energy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. “Unusually tired” is hard to judge because I feel like everyone at work is tired all the time, you know? The office has those fluorescent lights that make the afternoon feel longer than it is, and by the time I’m sorting emails or fixing someone’s travel itinerary for the third time, it’s like… of course I’m tired.\n\nBut over the past two weeks specifically? I haven’t really been measuring my energy. Some days I feel kind of drained, but I don’t know if it’s more than usual or just normal life catching up.\n\nDoctor: If the family situation had never happened, do you think you would still be feeling this same way?\n\nPatient: I think… probably not the same way, no. It’s hard to separate it out because it’s been tangled up with that for a while now.\n\nIt started after my family member’s health and behavior changed, and then I kind of became the person coordinating things—calls, appointments, checking in, keeping track of who said what. It’s funny, at work I’m good at coordinating calendars and details, so everyone assumes that means I can just do it endlessly in my personal life too, like I’m a walking spreadsheet.\n\nBut yeah… this has been going on about a year and three months, I’d say. Since that shift happened. So if none of that had happened, I don’t know that I’d be feeling like this, at least not in this same way.\n\nDoctor: Has this been making it hard for you to function at work or take care of things you need to do day to day?\n\nPatient: Some, yeah. Not like everything is falling apart. Work is mostly intact—I’m still getting the big things done, meetings still happen, people still get what they need. But I’ve noticed I miss little calendar details sometimes, or I’ll double-check something that used to be automatic, like whether I actually sent the attachment or put the right room number in.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve kind of pulled back. I used to do more photography outings, and I was mentoring a bit, but lately I don’t feel as present or reliable, so I’ve been saying no more than I used to. It’s not dramatic, but it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your loss of interest and heaviness are mainly a reaction to the ongoing family situation?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yeah, I think it’s tied to that ongoing family situation. Even keeping a clean calendar, which sounds boring, but that’s kind of my thing. When that started feeling like dragging furniture across carpet, I thought, okay, maybe this is more than just a bad week.\n\nDoctor: During this past year, have you often felt sad?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “sad” exactly, or at least not in a clean, obvious way. It’s more like… some days there’s this heaviness, and then other days I’m just going through the motions.\n\nSad is tricky, because when people say sad I picture crying at a movie or something clear-cut, and that’s not really how I’d describe it. Like if it’s an email or a scheduling thing, I’ll replay it in my head, and then I’ll remember some completely separate thing, like whether I put the parking receipt in the right folder, which sounds ridiculous out loud.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d say I can’t control it. Usually I can get pulled into something else if I have to. It’s more like my mind keeps circling back unless something interrupts me. Does that count as hard to control?\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt sad or depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “sad” most of the day. Depressed is such a heavy word, you know? I have days where I feel kind of low or like I’m moving through wet cement, but then I’ll get distracted by something—like reorganizing a meeting agenda or noticing the office printer is doing that thing where it leaves a line down the page—and then I’m not really thinking about my mood.\n\nSo… nearly every day, most of the day? I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to say yes if it’s not exactly that.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you felt down, depressed, or hopeless?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. “Hopeless” feels like a really specific word, and I don’t know that I’d use that. Down, maybe here and there, but then I get pulled into whatever’s in front of me—like a calendar issue or somebody needing a document reformatted—and then I’m just dealing with that.\n\nSo… in the past two weeks, I guess I’ve had moments of feeling down, but I don’t know if it’s enough to say yes the way you mean it.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you had much less interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s actually part of why I came in. The things that usually make me feel like myself have just felt kind of flat lately.\n\nLike, I usually like going out for photography walks—there’s this one street where the light hits the buildings in a really specific way around late afternoon, and I’ll take way too many pictures of the same fire escape, which sounds boring, but it’s calming for me. And mentoring the kids too, that usually matters to me. Even keeping my calendar clean, which I know is a weird thing to say I “enjoy,” but I do like when everything is organized.\n\nLately I just… don’t start those things. They feel heavy, like there’s no spark in them. I don’t know if it’s “much less pleasure” exactly, but yes, less interest, less pull toward doing them.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically tense or unable to relax?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “often.” I mean, I get tense sometimes, sure, but then I’m also sitting at a desk a lot, and those office chairs—there’s one in the conference room that sinks down slowly while you’re sitting in it, which drives me crazy because then you’re suddenly lower than everyone else at the table.\n\nBut physically tense… I haven’t really been tracking it. Unable to relax, maybe sometimes, but I’m not sure if that’s what you mean. I guess I’d have to think about it more.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you been feeling unusually tired or low on energy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. “Unusually tired” is hard to judge because I feel like everyone at work is tired all the time, you know? The office has those fluorescent lights that make the afternoon feel longer than it is, and by the time I’m sorting emails or fixing someone’s travel itinerary for the third time, it’s like… of course I’m tired.\n\nBut over the past two weeks specifically? I haven’t really been measuring my energy. Some days I feel kind of drained, but I don’t know if it’s more than usual or just normal life catching up.\n\nDoctor: If the family situation had never happened, do you think you would still be feeling this same way?\n\nPatient: I think… probably not the same way, no. It’s hard to separate it out because it’s been tangled up with that for a while now.\n\nIt started after my family member’s health and behavior changed, and then I kind of became the person coordinating things—calls, appointments, checking in, keeping track of who said what. It’s funny, at work I’m good at coordinating calendars and details, so everyone assumes that means I can just do it endlessly in my personal life too, like I’m a walking spreadsheet.\n\nBut yeah… this has been going on about a year and three months, I’d say. Since that shift happened. So if none of that had happened, I don’t know that I’d be feeling like this, at least not in this same way.\n\nDoctor: Has this been making it hard for you to function at work or take care of things you need to do day to day?\n\nPatient: Some, yeah. Not like everything is falling apart. Work is mostly intact—I’m still getting the big things done, meetings still happen, people still get what they need. But I’ve noticed I miss little calendar details sometimes, or I’ll double-check something that used to be automatic, like whether I actually sent the attachment or put the right room number in.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve kind of pulled back. I used to do more photography outings, and I was mentoring a bit, but lately I don’t feel as present or reliable, so I’ve been saying no more than I used to. It’s not dramatic, but it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your loss of interest and heaviness are mainly a reaction to the ongoing family situation?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yeah, I think it’s tied to that ongoing family situation. But what made me actually come in wasn’t just “I’m stressed,” because everybody says that and then you feel silly bringing it up.\n\nIt was more like… the things that usually make me feel like myself started feeling flat and heavy. Like photography walks—I’d usually notice the light or a weird reflection in a window, and lately I just don’t even start. And mentoring the kids, too, I’ve pulled back from initiating that. Even keeping a clean calendar, which sounds boring, but that’s kind of my thing. When that started feeling like dragging furniture across carpet, I thought, okay, maybe this is more than just a bad week.\n\nDoctor: During this past year, have you often felt sad?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “sad” exactly, or at least not in a clean, obvious way. It’s more like… some days there’s this heaviness, and then other days I’m just going through the motions.\n\nSad is tricky, because when people say sad I picture crying at a movie or something clear-cut, and that’s not really how I’d describe it. I mean, I’ll be sitting at my desk looking at the calendar color-coding, and suddenly I’m thinking about how I used to care about making things neat, and then I end up reorganizing paper clips instead of answering the question in my own head. So… often? Maybe. I’m not totally sure.\n\nDoctor: When the family situation eases up, do you still feel this same flatness and loss of interest?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, when things calm down a little with family, I’ll expect myself to sort of snap back, like, okay, now go take the camera out or text the mentoring group or clean up the calendar tabs. But it still feels weirdly flat.\n\nThat’s actually why I came in. The stuff that normally feels like “me”—photography walks, helping with the kids, even keeping my calendar neat—started feeling heavy, like something I had to push myself toward, and then I just stopped starting those things. I can get distracted by all the family noise, sure, but even when it’s quieter, I’m not really reaching for those things the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah, some nights. Not every single night, but there have been nights where I only sleep maybe two or three hours and then I’m up before dawn.\n\nAnd the weird part is I don’t always feel tired right away. I’ll wake up and my brain goes, okay, answer emails, update the appointment lists, fix that calendar thing—like it’s suddenly office hours at 4:30 in the morning. Yeah… sometimes, that’s the part that feels strange. If I only sleep two or three hours, I’ll wake up before dawn and instead of feeling wiped out, I’m like, okay, I can answer emails now, I can fix the appointment list, I can get ahead of things.

[
  {

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.23, F32.9, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess what brought me in is I don’t really feel like myself lately. The things I usually just do—like going on photography walks, helping mentor the kids, even keeping my calendar clean and organized—those used to kind of keep me grounded.\n\nNow they just feel… flat. Heavy. Like I look at the camera bag and think about going out, and then I don’t. Same with the mentoring stuff, and I used to be the person people came to for organizing things—funny, because that’s basically my job too—but lately I just stop before I start. I’m not initiating things the way I normally would, and that’s what made me think maybe I should come in.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Um… I’d say about a year and three months, give or take. It sort of started after a family member’s health and behavior changed, and I ended up taking on more of the coordinating—appointments, calls, keeping track of things. Which, I mean, I’m usually good at that kind of thing, that’s my whole work life too, but somewhere in there it started feeling different. So… yeah, roughly fifteen months.\n\nDoctor: Do these changes feel mainly driven by the situation with your family member?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, it lines up with that, timing-wise, so part of me says yes, probably. But then I think… lots of people deal with family stuff and still manage to keep their routines, you know?\n\nAnd it’s weird because organizing things is usually where I’m solid. Like at work, if someone needs a meeting moved or ten people coordinated, I can usually make that happen. I used to even color-code things—probably too much, honestly. My sister used to joke about it. So when it started feeling hard, I kind of blamed the family situation, but I don’t know if that explains all of it. It feels connected, but I can’t say it’s only that.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this down or flat most days for the past two years?\n\nPatient: Most days… yeah, I think flat is the better word than down. Like, not crying all the time or anything, but the stuff that normally makes me feel like me—going out with my camera, mentoring the kids, even just keeping my calendar neat—started feeling heavy.\n\nI just stopped starting things, if that makes sense. I’d look at the camera bag and then somehow end up sorting old emails instead, which is not even useful, just… I don’t know. But two years? I’m not sure it’s been that long. It feels more like it’s been going on a long while, but I don’t think a full two years.\n\nDoctor: Before this past 15 months, have you ever had another period lasting at least two weeks when you felt similarly flat or lost interest in the things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Before this past year or so? I don’t think I had it like this, not for two weeks straight where it really stood out. I mean, I’ve had times where I got bored with things—like I’d buy a camera lens and then not use it for a while, which my friend used to tease me about because I’d research it for weeks and then it would just sit there—but that wasn’t the same.\n\nThis time it’s more like the things that usually make me feel like myself, like going on photography walks, mentoring the kids, even just keeping my calendar clean… they started feeling flat and heavy. And I just stopped initiating them. That’s really what made me come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about a lot of different things?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it excessive. I mean, I worry about normal stuff—work things, whether I forgot to respond to someone, family things—but I’m not sure it’s “a lot of different things” in that way.\n\nSometimes I get stuck thinking about whether I handled something right, like if an email sounded too short. But then again, in my job, details kind of matter, so it’s hard to tell what’s worry and what’s just… being careful. What do you mean by excessive?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control the worrying once it starts?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, I guess? It depends what it’s about. Like if it’s an email or a scheduling thing, I’ll replay it in my head, and then I’ll remember some completely separate thing, like whether I put the parking receipt in the right folder, which sounds ridiculous out loud.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d say I can’t control it. Usually I can get pulled into something else if I have to. It’s more like my mind keeps circling back unless something interrupts me. Does that count as hard to control?\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt sad or depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “sad” most of the day. Depressed is such a heavy word, you know? I have days where I feel kind of low or like I’m moving through wet cement, but then I’ll get distracted by something—like reorganizing a meeting agenda or noticing the office printer is doing that thing where it leaves a line down the page—and then I’m not really thinking about my mood.\n\nSo… nearly every day, most of the day? I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to say yes if it’s not exactly that.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you felt down, depressed, or hopeless?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. “Hopeless” feels like a really specific word, and I don’t know that I’d use that. Down, maybe here and there, but then I get pulled into whatever’s in front of me—like a calendar issue or somebody needing a document reformatted—and then I’m just dealing with that.\n\nSo… in the past two weeks, I guess I’ve had moments of feeling down, but I don’t know if it’s enough to say yes the way you mean it.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you had much less interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s actually part of why I came in. The things that usually make me feel like myself have just felt kind of flat lately.\n\nLike, I usually like going out for photography walks—there’s this one street where the light hits the buildings in a really specific way around late afternoon, and I’ll take way too many pictures of the same fire escape, which sounds boring, but it’s calming for me. And mentoring the kids too, that usually matters to me. Even keeping my calendar clean, which I know is a weird thing to say I “enjoy,” but I do like when everything is organized.\n\nLately I just… don’t start those things. They feel heavy, like there’s no spark in them. I don’t know if it’s “much less pleasure” exactly, but yes, less interest, less pull toward doing them.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically tense or unable to relax?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “often.” I mean, I get tense sometimes, sure, but then I’m also sitting at a desk a lot, and those office chairs—there’s one in the conference room that sinks down slowly while you’re sitting in it, which drives me crazy because then you’re suddenly lower than everyone else at the table.\n\nBut physically tense… I haven’t really been tracking it. Unable to relax, maybe sometimes, but I’m not sure if that’s what you mean. I guess I’d have to think about it more.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you been feeling unusually tired or low on energy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. “Unusually tired” is hard to judge because I feel like everyone at work is tired all the time, you know? The office has those fluorescent lights that make the afternoon feel longer than it is, and by the time I’m sorting emails or fixing someone’s travel itinerary for the third time, it’s like… of course I’m tired.\n\nBut over the past two weeks specifically? I haven’t really been measuring my energy. Some days I feel kind of drained, but I don’t know if it’s more than usual or just normal life catching up.\n\nDoctor: If the family situation had never happened, do you think you would still be feeling this same way?\n\nPatient: I think… probably not the same way, no. It’s hard to separate it out because it’s been tangled up with that for a while now.\n\nIt started after my family member’s health and behavior changed, and then I kind of became the person coordinating things—calls, appointments, checking in, keeping track of who said what. It’s funny, at work I’m good at coordinating calendars and details, so everyone assumes that means I can just do it endlessly in my personal life too, like I’m a walking spreadsheet.\n\nBut yeah… this has been going on about a year and three months, I’d say. Since that shift happened. So if none of that had happened, I don’t know that I’d be feeling like this, at least not in this same way.\n\nDoctor: Has this been making it hard for you to function at work or take care of things you need to do day to day?\n\nPatient: Some, yeah. Not like everything is falling apart. Work is mostly intact—I’m still getting the big things done, meetings still happen, people still get what they need. But I’ve noticed I miss little calendar details sometimes, or I’ll double-check something that used to be automatic, like whether I actually sent the attachment or put the right room number in.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve kind of pulled back. I used to do more photography outings, and I was mentoring a bit, but lately I don’t feel as present or reliable, so I’ve been saying no more than I used to. It’s not dramatic, but it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your loss of interest and heaviness are mainly a reaction to the ongoing family situation?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yeah, I think it’s tied to that ongoing family situation. But what made me actually come in wasn’t just “I’m stressed,” because everybody says that and then you feel silly bringing it up.\n\nIt was more like… the things that usually make me feel like myself started feeling flat and heavy. Like photography walks—I’d usually notice the light or a weird reflection in a window, and lately I just don’t even start. And mentoring the kids, too, I’ve pulled back from initiating that. Even keeping a clean calendar, which sounds boring, but that’s kind of my thing. When that started feeling like dragging furniture across carpet, I thought, okay, maybe this is more than just a bad week.\n\nDoctor: During this past year, have you often felt sad?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “sad” exactly, or at least not in a clean, obvious way. It’s more like… some days there’s this heaviness, and then other days I’m just going through the motions.\n\nSad is tricky, because when people say sad I picture crying at a movie or something clear-cut, and that’s not really how I’d describe it. I mean, I’ll be sitting at my desk looking at the calendar color-coding, and suddenly I’m thinking about how I used to care about making things neat, and then I end up reorganizing paper clips instead of answering the question in my own head. So… often? Maybe. I’m not totally sure.\n\nDoctor: When the family situation eases up, do you still feel this same flatness and loss of interest?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, when things calm down a little with family, I’ll expect myself to sort of snap back, like, okay, now go take the camera out or text the mentoring group or clean up the calendar tabs. The stuff that normally feels like “me”—photography walks, helping with the kids, even keeping my calendar neat—started feeling heavy, like something I had to push myself toward, and then I just stopped starting those things. I can get distracted by all the family noise, sure, but even when it’s quieter, I’m not really reaching for those things the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah, some nights. Not every single night, but there have been nights where I only sleep maybe two or three hours and then I’m up before dawn.\n\nAnd the weird part is I don’t always feel tired right away. Which is ridiculous, because nobody needs an updated appointment list before sunrise, but there I am thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: When you sleep only two or three hours, do you still feel like you have plenty of energy the next day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes, that’s the part that feels strange. If I only sleep two or three hours, I’ll wake up before dawn and instead of feeling wiped out, I’m like, okay, I can answer emails now, I can fix the appointment list, I can get ahead of things.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking about whether the list should be sorted by date or by person, which is not really the point, but it feels important at the time. So yes, there are mornings where I feel like I’ve got enough energy, even though I barely slept.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

But I’ve noticed I miss little calendar details sometimes, or I’ll double-check something that used to be automatic, like whether I actually sent the attachment or put the right room number in.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve kind of pulled back. I used to do more photography outings, and I was mentoring a bit, but lately I don’t feel as present or reliable, so I’ve been saying no more than I used to. Like photography walks—I’d usually notice the light or a weird reflection in a window, and lately I just don’t even start. And mentoring the kids, too, I’ve pulled back from initiating that. Even keeping a clean calendar, which sounds boring, but that’s kind of my thing. When that started feeling like dragging furniture across carpet, I thought, okay, maybe this is more than just a bad week.\n\nDoctor: During this past year, have you often felt sad?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “sad” exactly, or at least not in a clean, obvious way. When that started feeling like dragging furniture across carpet, I thought, okay, maybe this is more than just a bad week.\n\nDoctor: During this past year, have you often felt sad?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “sad” exactly, or at least not in a clean, obvious way. I mean, I’ll be sitting at my desk looking at the calendar color-coding, and suddenly I’m thinking about how I used to care about making things neat, and then I end up reorganizing paper clips instead of answering the question in my own head.
